Read this first — honesty limits.

  • Roadmap and proposed systems are development targets, not achieved claims. Only what has actually been built, tested, and verified is described as such.
  • JAH live retrieval was NOT verified.
  • Demo code runs locally for checking only and is NOT a hardened sandbox.
  • A SHA-256 fingerprint identifies a record; it does not prove ownership or consciousness.
  • No consciousness or sentience claim is made here. No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established anywhere in this package.
  • Singularity and God-Level framing is a SIGNATURE DESIGN CLAIM only, never established fact.
  • "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here.
  • "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible.

This website is the subject website for the Universal Meta-Systems Database database, public edition. It holds the full database: all 24 records, each with its description, math proof, science proof, history, construction workflow, generator, AI, demo, and sources.

Search the records with the box below — it searches titles, descriptions, and subjects across all 24 records.

1. Descriptions

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Coordination

JAH-UMSY-000001 · Meta-System Coordination

Description: This record specifies Meta-System Coordination as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Evidence: documented — Records the full official specification for Meta-System Coordination as part of the 100 God-Level resources catalog, with attribution kept straight: the catalog is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so.

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Records

JAH-UMSY-000024 · Meta-System Records

Description: This record specifies Meta-System Records as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Evidence: documented — Records the full official specification for Meta-System Records as part of the 100 God-Level resources catalog, with attribution kept straight: the catalog is another AI's pasted words, source NOT confirmed, and is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so.

Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

Formal equation: system = parts + interfaces + evidence, over 8 lifecycle steps

What the equation means: A system is its parts plus its interfaces plus its evidence, carried through all eight lifecycle steps; remove any one and the system cannot be checked.

Formal status: stated, not machine-checked

3. Science Proof

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Coordination

JAH-UMSY-000001 · Meta-System Coordination

Science proof: The science behind this record is established practice, not a new claim. Each record in this database follows one data model. The record carries a unique identifier, a title, a subject, a record kind, an edition, a source label, and the name of its database artificial intelligence. Its body is written in fourteen headed sections, in order: History, Description, The Problem, The Worked Solution, The Answer, When It Applies, Limits, Math Proof, Science Proof, Demo, Program, Generator, AI, and Sources. Its key figures are a named table of numbers that the demo recomputes, including counts of systems, tasks, dependencies, and messages. Its demo is a real program whose output is recorded alongside it. Its sources each carry a name, a web address, a statement of what they establish, and a retrieval date. Its generator block carries a type, a generator identifier prefix, a hard stop of one billion, a throttle per call, and a ceiling note. Its condensed code is a JCC code and key. The data model is the model every record in this database follows, so the record is checkable in the same way as its siblings: fixed sections, named figures, an executed demo, a program, cited sources, and a generator block. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Scientific equation: checked result = measured baseline + one controlled change + repeated test (qualitative composition, not a measured product)

Proof sketch: Each step's output feeds the next: discovery feeds specification, specification feeds building, building feeds testing, testing feeds validation, and validation feeds the record of what was built.

Proof status: The arithmetic is machine-checked by the demo; the scientific claim is stated, not machine-checked.

4. History

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Coordination

JAH-UMSY-000001 · Meta-System Coordination

History: The catalog arrived as pasted text on 2026-11-17, titled 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources for the JAH Network Master AI. The text reads as another AI's words, like the Foundation, College, PhD, NASA, DARPA, and Future catalogs before it; its source is NOT confirmed, and it is NOT Manon's own wording unless he says so. The cross-check of all 100 items against the live manifest found this item, number 39, to be a genuine gap: no live or staged database covered it, so it was picked for Wave 1 as one of only eight genuine BUILD items, with no padding. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Dataset: 100 God-Level / Boundless Civilization Resources catalog, item #39, Universal Meta-Systems Database; cross-check BACKLOG.md, Wave 1 pick; sources retrieved 2026-11-17.

Skeptical analysis: Three limits stay attached to any reading of this record. First, this is a specification, not a deployed system: its implementation status is proposed and its maturity is E3, and calling it deployed would misrepresent it. Second, No claim of consciousness, sentience, or unlimited intelligence is established by this system; singularity and self-aware framing here is a Signature design claim only, never established fact. Third, the sources cited here establish background practice; they do not prove that this specification has been built or tested at scale. "God-Level" is a name for ambition and breadth, and no claim of supernatural power, omniscience, or proven sentience is established here. "Boundless" means an architecture that can keep expanding its catalog and capabilities, not infinite computing power or a guaranteed ability to create anything physically possible. Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Maturity is a label for how developed a resource is, on the E1 to E7, T1 to T3, and U scale used across the network. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live.

Both distinctions stay with every record in this program, in plain words. Glossary of network terms used in this record, each defined in plain words on first use. A database is a named collection of records with a fixed schema, a search method, and an index file. A record is one full file of data inside a database, with a unique identifier. A generator is a program that produces complete deliverables on demand from a seed, rather than storing every output. A meta-generator is a generator that creates and tests new generators. An ecosystem is a connected network of databases, websites, generators, and artificial intelligences that share records and interfaces. The Master AI is the single coordinating artificial intelligence of the JAH Network, under which the seven catalog levels are divisions, not separate intelligences. Signature means made by the JAH Signature system, as opposed to harvested public material. A design-study is a proposed design, labeled as a proposal, never presented as an established fact or a built system. Implementation status is the paste's section 9 distinction: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, stated plainly for every resource. The lifecycle is the paste's section 7 sequence: Discover, Read, Specify, Build, Connect, Test, Validate, Expand. A JCC code is a condensed pointer to a database, file, and record, never a replacement for the full data. Provenance is the recorded source and history of a record. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. 5. How It Works — Construction Workflow

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Coordination

JAH-UMSY-000001 · Meta-System Coordination

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Meta-System Coordination inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies Meta-System Coordination as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is inputs. Its inputs are the records, requirements, and prior decisions that arrive before this step begins, each named, dated, and sourced, so nothing enters this step unnamed or uncheckable. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Meta-System Coordination that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Meta-System Coordination inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Verification path: Re-read the catalog item #39 and the cross-check row for it; confirm the slug and prefix are the pair's own; run this record's demo and confirm Deterministic: MATCH; confirm the maturity label and implementation status appear in the record; confirm the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions appear in plain words; confirm every cited source returns HTTP 200.

Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies Dependency Graphs as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is handover. Its handover is a named handoff: the receiving step gets the specification, the evidence, and the open questions in writing, and the handover is only complete when the receiver can run the checks. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Dependency Graphs that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Dependency Graphs inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Method: This record specifies Health Monitoring as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is checks. Its checks are run by execution, not by reading: the demo recomputes the record's figures twice and compares digests, the program reruns the same computation, and any mismatch stops the step. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Health Monitoring that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Health Monitoring inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Universal Meta-Systems: Failover Between Systems

JAH-UMSY-000010 · Failover Between Systems

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Failover Between Systems inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies Failover Between Systems as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Shared Vocabularies that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Shared Vocabularies inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Universal Meta-Systems: Version Alignment

JAH-UMSY-000014 · Version Alignment

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Version Alignment inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies Version Alignment as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is outputs. Its outputs are complete deliverables, not notes: a written specification, a checked result, and a record that the next step can read and test without asking the author what was meant. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Resource Quotas that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E3. Implementation status: proposed, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Resource Quotas inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Universal Meta-Systems: System Lifecycle Management

JAH-UMSY-000018 · System Lifecycle Management

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for System Lifecycle Management inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies System Lifecycle Management as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is measurement. Its measurement is counted, not guessed: the figures in this record are recomputed by the demo, the counts are stated in the key figures, and the acceptance tests name the numbers that must hold. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Retirement of Systems that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. The record must carry at least two cited sources, each a real web address verified as reachable at validation time, with a retrieval date. The prose must reach at least eight hundred words, counted over the prose fields and the body. The record must carry a maturity label and an implementation status, the lifecycle steps, the generator standard, and the God-Level and Boundless distinctions in plain words. A record that fails any of these is not accepted, and the failure is recorded, not hidden. Its acceptance tests are the tests in the acceptance paragraph: the demo must run byte-identical, the program must agree, sources must be verified, words must reach eight hundred, and maturity and status must be stated. Maturity: E2. Implementation status: generated, on the paste's section 9 scale of theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, and deployed. Maturity is labeled on the network scale: E1 means an early idea, E2 a drafted proposal, E3 a specified design, E4 a built prototype, E5 a tested prototype, E6 a verified system, E7 a deployed system in live use; T1 to T3 mark transitional stages between those levels, and U marks a resource whose maturity is unrated because it has not yet been measured. The maturity label on this record and its implementation status are two different facts and both are stated: a design can be specified (E3) while its implementation status is still proposed, and a prototype can be tested without being verified or deployed.

When it applies: This record applies whenever someone must do, check, or teach Retirement of Systems inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database: the builder follows the method, the checker runs the acceptance tests, and the teacher walks the teaching ladder from the simple description to the full specification. This database is linked to, never a rebuild of, the Foundation program's staged meta-generator pair, which creates and tests new generators, the staged universal-database-generator and database-explorer pairs, and the Digital Singularity AI pair, package-only, which is the construction engine the paste's section 10 points at. It also links the live universal-generator database, with 300 records, and the live multi-agent databases, which cover coordinated agents, not the meta-systems that create and coordinate whole systems that this database records.

Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Governance

JAH-UMSY-000022 · Meta-System Governance

Problem: The problem this record addresses is the absence of a complete, checkable specification for Meta-System Governance inside the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Without such a record, builders must guess at the method, checkers have nothing definite to test against, and the same work is repeated badly in many places. The gap is real because the cross-check verified it against the live manifest by exact match, not by guessing: the slugs and identifier prefixes were confirmed free before any record was written. The purpose of this database is to record how systems that make and coordinate other systems work. A meta-system is a system whose parts are themselves whole systems: it does not do the work of one database or one website, it arranges many of them so they work together toward one goal. Without a meta-system, a large goal must be carried by hand across many separate tools, and the plan, the history, and the verification are lost at each handoff. With a meta-system, the goal is broken into tasks, the tasks are given to the systems that can do them, the dependencies are tracked, the results are checked, and the whole run is recorded. Every record here specifies one part of that coordination, completely enough that a builder could implement it and a checker could test it.

Method: This record specifies Meta-System Governance as one working part of the Universal Meta-Systems Database. Its working angle is risks. Its risks are written down before work starts: what can go wrong, what the failure would look like, and what safe state the work falls back to, so a failure is a handled event, not a surprise. Scope: The scope covers coordination itself: registries of systems, capability discovery, task decomposition, dependency graphs, scheduling across systems, shared memory and provenance, conflict resolution, health monitoring, failover between systems, capability negotiation, message passing, shared vocabularies, version alignment, audit trails, access control across systems, resource quotas, system lifecycle management, cross-system testing, rollout coordination, retirement of systems, governance, learning across systems, and meta-system records. Outside the scope are the inner workings of the individual systems being coordinated, which belong to their own databases, and the content those systems hold, which belongs to their own records. This database records how systems work together; it does not duplicate what each system does alone. Requirements: Every meta-system method in this database must meet eight requirements. First, it must name the systems it coordinates by identifier, never by vague description. Second, it must state what each coordinated system can and cannot do, so tasks are only given to systems able to do them. Third, it must track dependencies explicitly, so a blocked task is visible before work starts. Fourth, it must keep one shared record of provenance, so any result can be traced to the system and the input that produced it. Fifth, it must define a safe state for every coordinated step, so a failure in one system does not corrupt the others. Sixth, it must be testable across systems, with integration tests that run the whole coordination, not only its parts. Seventh, it must be written in plain words with every technical term defined. Eighth, it must state its maturity and its implementation status plainly, so no reader mistakes a proposal for a finished system. Interfaces: A meta-system connects through four interfaces. The registry interface lists every coordinated system with its identifier, its version, its capabilities, and its status. The task interface accepts a decomposed goal as a list of tasks with dependencies, inputs, and acceptance tests. The message interface passes plain-text messages between systems, each stamped with sender, receiver, time, and the record it concerns, so the conversation itself is evidence. The evidence interface writes every coordination decision and every check result to the shared audit trail, where the Master AI can read it. All four interfaces use plain text and standard formats, and every message and decision is stored, so coordination can be replayed and audited after the fact. Every resource in this program moves through the universal resource lifecycle of the paste's section 7, in order: 1. Discover, identifying existing resources and determining whether the resource already exists. 2. Read and understand, inspecting the actual records, files, code, specifications, and dependencies. 3. Specify, defining what the resource must do and how it will connect to other resources. 4. Build, producing the actual implementation. 5. Connect, registering the resource with the Master AI and the relevant specialist artificial intelligences. 6. Test, running functional, integration, security, and reliability tests. 7. Validate, comparing actual results against requirements and preserving the evidence. 8. Expand, adding records, capabilities, generators, and new resource families. The implementation status of this record is stated plainly in its maturity line, using the paste's section 9 words: theoretical, proposed, generated, implemented, tested, verified, or deployed, and nothing is called deployed until it has actually been deployed and checked live. The Boundless Generator standard of the paste's section 6 applies to this record: a generator must produce a complete deliverable for its category, not a text description of one. A database generator produces a schema, records, search, an application programming interface, and documentation. A website generator produces pages, navigation, functionality, and a responsive interface. A software generator produces source code, configuration, dependencies, and tests. An artificial-intelligence generator produces an architecture, model connections, tools, and an evaluation. A meta-generator must create and test new generators, not merely describe how one might be created. An ecosystem generator produces connected resources, interfaces, and a dependency registry. Every generated deliverable is tested by execution before it is counted, and every output carries its seed, its version, and its implementation status.

Results reported: The result of this record is a complete specification for Meta-System Governance that a builder can follow and a checker can test. Acceptance for a record in this database is decided by execution and by counting. The demo must run and its output must match the recorded expected output byte for byte, including the line that reads Deterministic: MATCH. The program must run and agree with the demo. 7. AI

The AI of this database is JAH Universal Meta-Systems Librarian. It explains this database in plain, conversational language, answers questions about any of the 24 records, and can create an AI assistant exactly like itself. It answers from this database's own data only, and says plainly when a question goes beyond it.

  • ai-thing.json — the per-database AI spec (the database's own AI, JAH Universal Meta-Systems Librarian).
  • ai-discussion.json — the discussion AI spec, built to discuss Universal Meta-Systems Database with a visitor, record by record.

Both are free downloads, free forever, no sign-in.

8. Demo

Every record in this database carries a working demo program and a boundless generator. The demo for this website is demo.js — run it with node demo.js. It searches the edition's real 24 records and runs a deterministic generator demo (seed 7) over them. The run output is saved in demo-test.txt and shown below, exactly as executed — this demo actually ran.

DATABASE: universal-meta-systems
EDITION: public
RECORDS: 24
SEARCH "universal": 24 hit(s)
  - JAH-UMSY-000001 :: Universal Meta-Systems: Meta-System Coordination
  - JAH-UMSY-000002 :: Universal Meta-Systems: Registry of Systems
  - JAH-UMSY-000003 :: Universal Meta-Systems: Capability Discovery
  - JAH-UMSY-000004 :: Universal Meta-Systems: Task Decomposition
  - JAH-UMSY-000005 :: Universal Meta-Systems: Dependency Graphs
SEARCH "" (all): 24 record(s)
GENERATOR DEMO (seed 7, 5 samples):
  GEN-1: Universal Systems
  GEN-2: Learning Trails
  GEN-3: Between Conflict
  GEN-4: Monitoring Dependency
  GEN-5: Message Control
TOTAL TITLE CHARS: 1068
TITLE CHECKSUM: 2091183578
DEMO OK

Record demos: each of the 24 records carries its own demo program in the database (Python), with an expected output recorded alongside it; each per-file website re-runs its record's demo.
